Use when asked about GPU selectors, attributes, capacity, sharing, passthrough, scheduling, allocation failures, or container-visible devices.\n---\n\n# DRA GPU Allocation Workflows\n\n## Purpose\n\nTrace a GPU request from declared intent through scheduler allocation and node preparation to the exact device, sharing, and CDI result.\n\n## Triggers\n\n- Explain or create a GPU, MIG, VFIO, time-slicing, or MPS claim.\n- Diagnose Pending pods, unallocated claims, prepare/unprepare failures, missing devices, or stale inventory.\n- Review DeviceClasses, ResourceSlice attributes/capacity, CEL selectors, opaque configuration, or feature-gate constraints.\n- Analyze dynamic MIG lifecycle, checkpoint recovery, or CDI injection.\n\n## Audience\n\n- Contributors\n- Cluster operators\n- Support engineers\n\n## Inputs\n\n- Driver/chart and Kubernetes versions\n- GPU model, MIG/VFIO state, NVIDIA driver, runtime/CDI, and feature gates\n- ResourceClaim or ResourceClaimTemplate, Pod, DeviceClass, and ResourceSlice output\n- Pod/claim events, gpu-kubelet-plugin logs, checkpoint symptoms, and container evidence\n- Whether GPU Operator manages the surrounding stack\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. Verify current behavior from `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md`, the relevant guide/reference pages, and code. Do not extrapolate planned release behavior.\n2. Classify the requested resource:\n   - Full GPU: `gpu.nvidia.com`\n   - MIG slice: `mig.nvidia.com`\n   - VFIO passthrough: `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com`\n3. Identify the sharing/config contract from `api/nvidia.com/resource/v1beta1/`:\n   - `GpuConfig`\n   - `MigDeviceConfig`\n   - `VfioDeviceConfig`\n   - Sharing and IOMMU settings\n4. Validate required feature gates and every dependency or mutual exclusion against `pkg/featuregates/featuregates.go` and current docs. Treat code/docs differences as drift to report.\n5. Inspect the matching DeviceClass templates and the node's `gpu.nvidia.com` ResourceSlice. Distinguish serialized bare attribute keys from CEL access through `device.attributes['gpu.nvidia.com']` and capacity through `device.capacity['gpu.nvidia.com']`.\n6. Trace the allocation path:\n   - Pod references a ResourceClaim or template.\n   - The scheduler matches DeviceClass, CEL selectors, attributes, and capacity against ResourceSlices and records the allocation/node.\n   - Kubelet calls the GPU plugin's prepare path.\n   - The plugin validates config and device state, performs any MIG/VFIO/sharing setup, persists prepared state, writes CDI specs, and returns CDI device names.\n   - The runtime consumes CDI and starts the container.\n   - Unprepare reverses driver-owned state when the claim is released.\n7. For static MIG, confirm partitions existed before plugin discovery and restart the plugin after approved out-of-band hardware changes. For dynamic MIG, verify advertised partitionable capacity, Kubernetes gate/version requirements, on-demand creation, teardown, and checkpoint recovery.\n8. For VFIO, verify IOMMU, driver binding, feature gates, workload/runtime expectations, and processes that may hold the GPU. Treat host driver changes as privileged operations.\n9. For sharing, distinguish time-slicing, MPS, MIG hardware isolation, and plain multi-container use of the same claim. Do not describe them as equivalent isolation models.\n10. Diagnose in layers: DeviceClass → ResourceSlice inventory/health → claim allocation → scheduler events → node assignment → plugin prepare → checkpoint/CDI → runtime/container.\n11. Use maintained samples under `demo/specs/` and docs under `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/`; adapt API versions to the target Kubernetes release.\n12. Return a request-to-injection explanation or bounded triage report with prerequisites, observed evidence, unsupported assumptions, and validation steps.\n\n## Expected Outputs\n\n- GPU claim or selector design\n- Request-to-CDI flow explanation\n- Allocation triage report\n- Compatibility and validation checklist\n\n## References\n\n- GPU concepts: `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md`\n- API types: `site/content/docs/reference/api.md`, `api/nvidia.com/resource/v1beta1/`\n- ResourceSlice fields and CEL names: `site/content/docs/reference/resourceslice-attributes.md`\n- Feature gates: `site/content/docs/reference/feature-gates.md`, `pkg/featuregates/featuregates.go`\n- Task guides: `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/`\n- Implementation: `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/`\n- Samples and tests: `demo/specs/`, `test/e2e/`, `tests/bats/`\n- Public Kubernetes DRA concepts: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/scheduling-eviction/dynamic-resource-allocation/\n- Public GPU Operator DRA guide: https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/cloud-native/gpu-operator/latest/dra-intro-install.html\n- Internal supplemental: none provided by this skill\n\n## Validation\n\n- Identify DeviceClass, requested device count, selectors, opaque config, feature gates, and expected sharing/isolation semantics.\n- Confirm the allocated device and node from claim status and ResourceSlice data.\n- Confirm prepare succeeded and a CDI device reached the intended container.\n- For the ADR scenario, explain ResourceClaim → scheduler binding → kubelet prepare → CDI injection and name the evidence available at each boundary.\n- For dynamic MIG, verify actual partition creation and later teardown, not only scheduler allocation.\n- For failures, separate driver behavior from Kubernetes scheduler, GPU Operator, NVIDIA driver, runtime/CDI, and hardware configuration.\n\n## Safety Constraints\n\n- Use read-only cluster inspection first and obtain explicit approval before applying claims, restarting plugins, changing MIG/VFIO state, editing host drivers, deleting checkpoints, or running invasive tests.\n- Never expose kubeconfigs, credentials, GPU customer data, or full sensitive logs.\n- Preserve active workloads and claims when suggesting recovery.\n\n## Non-Goals\n\n- Do not claim unsupported hardware, feature gates, or releases are production-ready.\n- Do not confuse DRA with the legacy extended-resource device-plugin path.\n- Do not treat time-slicing or MPS as MIG-equivalent hardware isolation.\n\n## Owners\n\n- DRA GPU engineering\n- DRA support\n\n## Refresh Policy\n\n- Review every DRA driver release and Kubernetes minor bump.\n- Review after GPU API, ResourceSlice attribute, feature-gate, MIG, VFIO, checkpoint, CDI, or GPU Operator changes.\n- Verify current public prerequisites and known issues before operator-facing guidance.\n\n## Registration Notes\n\nKeep this `SKILL.md` as the shared source of truth. Use `agents/openai.yaml` only as registration metadata.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

Each slice has its own dedicated compute engines, memory bandwidth, and L2 cache — workloads are fully isolated at the hardware level.\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:41:Unlike time-slicing and MPS, MIG partitioning is enforced by the GPU hardware itself. A container claiming a MIG slice cannot be affected by activity on another slice of the same physical GPU.\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:43:MIG slices are identified by profiles such as `1g.5gb` or `3g.20gb`, where the first number is the fraction of GPU compute and the second is the dedicated memory allocation. The available profiles depend on the physical GPU model.\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:45:#### Static and dynamic MIG\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:47:MIG support has two modes:\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:49:- **Static MIG:** the node administrator pre-configures MIG partitions using\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:53:- **Dynamic MIG:** the driver creates and destroys partitions automatically\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:54:  based on workload requests. The kubelet plugin generates a CDI spec at prepare time; the container runtime reads it to set up the device files, environment variables, and mounts.\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:195:## Publishing GPUs in ResourceSlices\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:197:The GPU kubelet plugin discovers the GPUs on each node and publishes them as `ResourceSlice` objects. One pool per node, all under the `gpu.nvidia.com` driver. The make up of a `ResourceSlice` depends on how each GPU is configured:\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:199:- A GPU available as a full device is published with device `type: gpu` and you can request it through the `gpu.nvidia.com` DeviceClass.\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:200:- A GPU with MIG mode enabled is published as its MIG slices instead of a full GPU. Each MIG slice is assigned `type: mig` and you can request it through the `mig.nvidia.com` DeviceClass. With dynamic MIG, the driver also advertises every possible MIG partition plus per-GPU shared counters. See [How dynamic MIG works](#how-dynamic-mig-works) above.\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:201:- A GPU prepared for VFIO passthrough is published with device `type: vfio` and you can request it through the `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com` DeviceClass.\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:203:The driver discovers the node's GPUs once, when the GPU kubelet plugin starts, and that inventory stays fixed while the plugin runs. It republishes `ResourceSlices` at runtime to reflect allocation, as devices are claimed and released, and, if `NVMLDeviceHealthCheck` is enabled, device health changes. The GPU kubelet plugin does not re-scan hardware. If you make a manual change to a node's GPU or MIG configuration, for example, reconfiguring MIG with `nvidia-smi`, you must restart the GPU kubelet plugin for the changes to take effect in the resource pool.\nsite/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:205:The `ResourceSlices` contain the attributes and capacity details for all the GPUs on your cluster. This page explains what they are, how they differ, and how Kubernetes schedules them.\n\n| DeviceClass | Resource type | Use case |\n|---|---|---|\n| `gpu.nvidia.com` | Full GPU | Exclusive or shared access to a single physical GPU |\n| `mig.nvidia.com` | MIG slice | Hardware-isolated partition of a supported GPU |\n| `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com` | VFIO passthrough | Raw GPU access for workloads that manage the driver themselves |\n\n---\n\n## Resource types\n\n### Full GPUs\n\nA full GPU gives a container exclusive access to a single physical GPU. This is the default allocation mode and requires no additional configuration.\n\nFull GPUs support optional sharing for cases where you want to divide the GPU across multiple workloads:\n\n- **Consumable capacity:** independent `ResourceClaim` objects can share a multi-allocatable device while Kubernetes tracks the capacity consumed by each claim.\n  The NVIDIA driver requires its `ConsumableShares` feature gate and a `consumableShares` mode.\n- **Time-slicing:** containers take turns on the GPU using CUDA preemption. Requires the `TimeSlicingSettings` feature gate.\n- **MPS (Multi-Process Service):** containers run concurrently with configurable thread percentage and memory limits. Requires the `MPSSupport` feature gate.\n\nTarget DeviceClass: `gpu.nvidia.com`\n\nSee [`demo/specs/quickstart/`](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/tree/{{< param driver_release_tag >}}/demo/specs/quickstart) for configuration examples.\n\n---\n\n### MIG slices\n\nMIG (Multi-Instance GPU) partitions a MIG-capable NVIDIA data center GPU (such as A100, A30, H100, and newer) into independent hardware slices. Each slice has its own dedicated compute engines, memory bandwidth, and L2 cache — workloads are fully isolated at the hardware level.\n\nUnlike time-slicing and MPS, MIG partitioning is enforced by the GPU hardware itself. A container claiming a MIG slice cannot be affected by activity on another slice of the same physical GPU.\n\nMIG slices are identified by profiles such as `1g.5gb` or `3g.20gb`, where the first number is the fraction of GPU compute and the second is the dedicated memory allocation. The available profiles depend on the physical GPU model.\n\n#### Static and dynamic MIG\n\nMIG support has two modes:\n\n- **Static MIG:** the node administrator pre-configures MIG partitions using\n  `mig-parted` or `nvidia-smi`. The driver discovers existing partitions and\n  exposes them as allocatable devices. This is enabled by default; no feature\n  gate is required.\n- **Dynamic MIG:** the driver creates and destroys partitions automatically\n  based on workload requests. Requires the `DynamicMIG` feature gate.\n\nStatic and dynamic MIG differ only in *who* creates the partitions, not in how\nyou request them. The same `ResourceClaimTemplate` and pod manifests work in\nboth modes.\n\n#### How static MIG discovery works\n\nStatic MIG requires no feature gate and no MIG-specific driver configuration.\nThe driver always discovers pre-configured MIG partitions, so a default install\n(`resources.gpus.enabled=true`) is all that is needed on the driver side. All of\nthe setup happens on the node, before the driver starts.\n\nThe GPU kubelet plugin discovers partitions at startup and advertises each one as\na device in the node's `ResourceSlice`:\n\n- Discovery is a point-in-time snapshot. Partitions added or changed after the\n  driver starts are not picked up until the GPU kubelet plugin restarts.\n- MIG mode enabled with no partitions strands the GPU. If a GPU has MIG mode\n  enabled but no partitions configured, the driver advertises neither the full\n  GPU nor any slices for it, and logs a warning.\n\n#### How dynamic MIG works\n\nWith dynamic MIG, the driver creates and destroys partitions automatically based\non workload requests, so you do not pre-configure them on the node.\n\nDynamic MIG builds on the Kubernetes partitionable devices feature (KEP-4815).\nIt works in three phases:\n\n1. **Advertise every possible partition.** At startup, the GPU kubelet plugin\n   enumerates all MIG profiles and every valid placement for each profile on\n   each GPU, then advertises all of them in the node's `ResourceSlice`, even\n   though none exist on the hardware yet. Alongside the devices, it publishes\n   shared counters (one set per physical GPU) that track compute, memory, and\n   memory-slice usage. Each advertised partition declares how much of those\n   counters it would consume. This lets the scheduler allocate a partition that\n   does not yet exist, and prevents handing out two partitions that would\n   physically overlap on the same GPU.\n2. **Create the partition on demand.** After the scheduler allocates a partition\n   to a pod, the GPU kubelet plugin creates the actual MIG device when it\n   prepares the pod's resources on the node — enabling MIG mode on the GPU if\n   needed, then creating the GPU instance and compute instance for the requested\n   profile and placement.\n3. **Destroy the partition when it is no longer needed.** When the claim is\n   released, the driver tears the MIG device back down. The plugin uses a\n   node-local checkpoint as the source of truth, so it can reclaim partitions\n   left behind by a crash or partial preparation when it restarts.\n\nA request can be satisfied even when no matching partition was pre-configured —\nthe driver creates one to match.\n\nMIG slices support consumable capacity, time-slicing, and MPS sharing, using the same approaches as full GPUs.\nTime-slicing on MIG slices does not support interval configuration.\n\nTarget DeviceClass: `mig.nvidia.com`\n\nSee the [Request full GPUs guide](../guides/gpu-allocation/allocating-gpus.md) and\n[`demo/specs/quickstart/`](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/tree/{{< param driver_release_tag >}}/demo/specs/quickstart)\nfor GPU configuration examples. For MIG setup and workload examples, see the [MIG guide](../guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md).\n\n---\n\n### VFIO passthrough\n\nVFIO (Virtual Function I/O) passes a full physical GPU directly to a container, bypassing the NVIDIA driver stack in the host kernel. This gives the container raw hardware access and is intended for workloads that manage the GPU driver themselves, such as virtual machine managers or specialized research environments.\n\nVFIO passthrough has no sharing options; one container gets one GPU.\n\nTarget DeviceClass: `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com`\n\nRequires the `PassthroughSupport` feature gate (Alpha, default: false). See [Feature gates](../reference/feature-gates.md) to enable it.\n\n---\n\n## Choosing a resource type\n\n| | Full GPU | MIG slice | VFIO passthrough |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Hardware isolation | No | Yes | Full device |\n| Sharing supported | Yes (consumable capacity, time-slicing, or MPS) | Yes (consumable capacity, time-slicing, or MPS) | No |\n| Supported hardware | All NVIDIA GPUs | MIG-capable data center GPUs (A100, A30, H100, and newer) | All NVIDIA GPUs |\n| Feature gate required | No (sharing gates optional) | No | `PassthroughSupport` (Alpha) |\n| Typical use case | ML training, general workloads | Multi-tenant inference, strict isolation | VM passthrough, specialized environments |\n\n### Add optional allocation features\n\nChoose a resource type first, and then add only the features that apply to that\nresource:\n\n- **Sharing across claims:** Kubernetes DRA consumable capacity lets independent claims share a full GPU or MIG device by memory capacity, without a capacity limit, or by a fixed share count.\n  Refer to [Consumable capacity](../guides/gpu-allocation/consumable-capacity.md).\n- **Sharing within a claim:** time-slicing or MPS can share supported full-GPU or MIG allocations among containers that reference the claim.\n  A device configuration uses one sharing strategy.\n- **Fabric topology:** Fabric Manager partitioning constrains a claim to one\n  complete NVSwitch partition.\n  The partitioning applies to full GPUs and VFIO devices, not MIG slices.\n  `PassthroughSupport` is required if you want to use VFIO devices.\n- **Operational features:** health checking and device metadata modify\n  supported workflows without creating another resource type.\n\nRefer to [Feature gate constraints](../reference/feature-gates.md#constraints)\nfor the combinations enforced at driver startup.\nFor the topology workflow, refer to\n[Fabric Manager partitioning](../guides/gpu-allocation/fabric-manager-partitioning.md).\n\n---\n\n## How scheduling works\n\nWhen a pod references a `ResourceClaim`, Kubernetes uses the following flow to allocate and inject the GPU:\n\n```mermaid\nsequenceDiagram\n    participant User\n    participant API as Kubernetes API\n    participant Scheduler\n    participant Kubelet\n    participant Plugin as GPU Kubelet Plugin\n    participant Runtime as Container Runtime\n\n    User->>API: Create Pod + ResourceClaim\n    API->>Scheduler: Schedule pod\n    Scheduler->>Plugin: Query available ResourceSlices\n    Scheduler->>API: Bind ResourceClaim to device + node\n    API->>Kubelet: Start pod on bound node\n    Kubelet->>Plugin: NodePrepareResources (claim + device + config)\n    Plugin->>Plugin: Generate CDI spec\n    Plugin->>Kubelet: Return CDI device names\n    Kubelet->>Runtime: Start container with CDI devices\n    Runtime->>User: Container running with GPU\n```\n\nThe key components in this flow:\n\n- **ResourceSlice:** the kubelet plugin publishes each node's GPU devices in a single pool (one or more ResourceSlices), advertising the devices it makes allocatable and their attributes.\n- **ResourceClaim:** the user's request for a specific type of GPU device, optionally with a configuration (GpuConfig, MigDeviceConfig, or VfioDeviceConfig) embedded as opaque parameters.\n- **CDI (Container Device Interface):** the standard used to inject devices into containers. The kubelet plugin generates a CDI spec at prepare time; the container runtime reads it to set up the device files, environment variables, and mounts.\n\n---\n\n## Publishing GPUs in ResourceSlices\n\nThe GPU kubelet plugin discovers the GPUs on each node and publishes them as `ResourceSlice` objects. One pool per node, all under the `gpu.nvidia.com` driver. The make up of a `ResourceSlice` depends on how each GPU is configured:\n\n- A GPU available as a full device is published with device `type: gpu` and you can request it through the `gpu.nvidia.com` DeviceClass.\n- A GPU with MIG mode enabled is published as its MIG slices instead of a full GPU. Each MIG slice is assigned `type: mig` and you can request it through the `mig.nvidia.com` DeviceClass. With dynamic MIG, the driver also advertises every possible MIG partition plus per-GPU shared counters. See [How dynamic MIG works](#how-dynamic-mig-works) above.\n- A GPU prepared for VFIO passthrough is published with device `type: vfio` and you can request it through the `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com` DeviceClass.\n\nThe driver discovers the node's GPUs once, when the GPU kubelet plugin starts, and that inventory stays fixed while the plugin runs. It republishes `ResourceSlices` at runtime to reflect allocation, as devices are claimed and released, and, if `NVMLDeviceHealthCheck` is enabled, device health changes. The GPU kubelet plugin does not re-scan hardware. If you make a manual change to a node's GPU or MIG configuration, for example, reconfiguring MIG with `nvidia-smi`, you must restart the GPU kubelet plugin for the changes to take effect in the resource pool.\n\nThe `ResourceSlices` contain the attributes and capacity details for all the GPUs on your cluster. To review the `ResourceSlices` on your cluster, see [View available GPU resources](../guides/gpu-allocation/view-resources.md); for what each attribute and capacity field means, see [ResourceSlice device attributes](../reference/resourceslice-attributes.md).\n\n\n## Requesting a GPU\n\nResource request contains the following information:\n\n*  A `ResourceClaimTemplate` (or `ResourceClaim`) references one of the available DeviceClasses\n* Optionally, CEL selectors match the GPU attributes your workload requires\n* Optionally, an opaque driver configuration block for `GpuConfig`, `MigDeviceConfig`, or `VfioDeviceConfig` sharing and device behavior.\n\nThe scheduler uses these details to pick a matching device from the node's ResourceSlice.\n\nRefer to the [Kubernetes DRA documentation](https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/scheduling-eviction/dynamic-resource-allocation/) for details on the  claim and templating mechanics.\n\nFor more details on using these with the DRA, refer to the how-to guides:\n\n---DOC SPLIT---\n---\ntitle: MIG (Multi-Instance GPU)\nlinkTitle: MIG\nweight: 45\naliases:\n  - /docs/guides/mig/\n  - /docs/guides/dynamic-mig/\n  - /docs/guides/gpu-allocation/dynamic-mig/\ndescription: Allocate MIG slices to containers using static or dynamic MIG.\n---\n\nMIG lets you partition a single NVIDIA GPU into multiple isolated GPU instances,\neach with a fixed allocation of compute resources, memory, and memory bandwidth.\nUnlike time-slicing, MIG instances have hardware-level isolation. Each\ninstance's memory and compute are fully separate.\n\nUse MIG when you need predictable, isolated GPU resources for multiple concurrent\nworkloads on the same physical GPU. For how MIG compares to time-slicing and the\nother sharing modes, refer to\n[Choosing a resource type](../../concepts/gpu-allocation.md#choosing-a-resource-type).\n\nEach MIG instance is exposed under the `mig.nvidia.com` DeviceClass and carries\nthe `gpu.nvidia.com/type` attribute value `mig`. Beyond the parent-GPU\nattributes shared with full GPUs, MIG devices add a `profile` (for example\n`1g.5gb`) and a `parentUUID`, and advertise per-slice capacity such as `memory`\nand `multiprocessors`.\n\nFor the full list of MIG attributes and capacity you can match with CEL\nselectors, refer to\n[ResourceSlice device attributes](../../reference/resourceslice-attributes.md#mig-slice-type-mig).\n\nThis guide covers static and dynamic MIG setup and workload examples. For how\nstatic and dynamic MIG work in the driver, refer to\n[Static and dynamic MIG](../../concepts/gpu-allocation.md#static-and-dynamic-mig)\non the GPU allocation concept page. For how to inspect ResourceSlice\nobjects, refer to [View available GPU resources](view-resources.md). For\nhow to write CEL selectors, refer to\n[Request full GPUs](allocating-gpus.md#select-a-gpu-by-product-name).\n\n## Dynamic MIG feature status\n\n| Feature gate | Default | Stage |\n|---|---|---|\n| `DynamicMIG` | `false` | Alpha |\n\n`DynamicMIG` is mutually exclusive with the following feature gates. The driver\nreturns a validation error at startup if any of these are enabled together:\n\n- `PassthroughSupport`\n- `NVMLDeviceHealthCheck`\n- `MPSSupport`\n\nRefer to the [feature gates reference](../../reference/feature-gates.md) for all\navailable gates.\n\nStatic MIG support is enabled by default; no feature gate is required.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- The DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs must be installed. Refer to [Installation](../../install.md).\n- One or more GPUs in your cluster must support MIG. MIG is available on NVIDIA\n  data center GPUs with the Ampere architecture or newer. For the list of\n  supported GPUs, refer to the\n  [NVIDIA MIG User Guide](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/tesla/mig-user-guide/#supported-gpus).\n- For static MIG, create the MIG partitions on the node before the driver starts.\n  Refer to [Configure static MIG](#configure-static-mig).\n- For dynamic MIG, enable the `DynamicMIG` feature gate. Refer to\n  [Enabling dynamic MIG](#enabling-dynamic-mig).\n\n\n## Enabling dynamic MIG\n\nEnable the `DynamicMIG` feature gate to use dynamic MIG. For how partition\ncreation and teardown work, refer to\n[How dynamic MIG works](../../concepts/gpu-allocation.md#how-dynamic-mig-works).\n\nEnable `DynamicMIG` with `helm upgrade --install`. This installs the driver if\nit is not already present, or upgrades an existing release:\n\n```bash\nhelm upgrade --install dra-driver-nvidia-gpu oci://registry.k8s.io/dra-driver-nvidia/charts/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu \\\n  --namespace dra-driver-nvidia-gpu --create-namespace \\\n  --set featureGates.DynamicMIG=true \\\n  --set gpuResourcesEnabledOverride=true\n```\n\nThe GPU kubelet plugin must restart for the change to take effect. The rolling\nupdate happens automatically when you upgrade the Helm release.\n\nDynamic MIG depends on the Kubernetes partitionable devices feature (KEP-4815).\nOn Kubernetes v1.34–v1.35, you must also enable the `DRAPartitionableDevices`\nfeature gate on the kube-apiserver and kube-scheduler. This feature gate is\nrequired to allow the scheduler to allocate dynamically created MIG devices. On\nKubernetes v1.36 and later, `DRAPartitionableDevices` is enabled by default and\nno action is required.\n\n## Limitations and considerations\n\n- MIG is available only on NVIDIA data center GPUs with the Ampere architecture\n  or newer. Available profiles depend on the GPU model. For supported GPUs and\n  profiles, refer to the\n  [NVIDIA MIG User Guide](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/tesla/mig-user-guide/#supported-gpus).\n- TimeSlicing has no effect on MIG devices. Setting `strategy: TimeSlicing` in a\n  `MigDeviceConfig` does not affect hardware behavior. To share a MIG slice\n  across containers, use Multi-Process Service (MPS) instead with the\n  `MPSSupport` feature gate. Note that `MPSSupport` is mutually exclusive with\n  `DynamicMIG`; they cannot be used together.\n- Static MIG partitions must be configured before the driver starts. In static\n  mode, the driver does not modify the node's MIG configuration. Partitions added\n  after the driver starts are not discovered until the GPU kubelet plugin\n  restarts.\n- Dynamic MIG replaces static discovery. On a MIG-capable node, the driver\n  manages partitions itself and does not use pre-created static partitions.\n  MIG partitions the driver did not create are destroyed when the GPU kubelet\n  plugin starts, so do not enable `DynamicMIG` on nodes with partitions you want\n  to keep.\n- On dynamic MIG nodes, let the driver\n  create and destroy partitions through workload requests. Do not run\n  `mig-parted` or `nvidia-smi mig` while the GPU kubelet plugin is running, because\n  manual changes can conflict with the driver's partition state and cause pod\n  preparation or cleanup to fail.\n- On Ampere GPUs (for example, A100), enable MIG mode on the GPU before the\n  DRA Driver starts. Ampere cannot toggle MIG mode without a GPU reset. If MIG\n  mode is off, the driver falls back to full-GPU allocation and advertises no\n  MIG partitions. Hopper and later architectures enable MIG mode on demand.\n- Dynamic MIG is not supported on vGPU guests.\n\n## Examples\n\nThe following examples work with static or dynamic MIG.\n\n### Create the example namespace\n\nThe examples use the `mig-example` namespace. Create it first:\n\n```bash\nkubectl create namespace mig-example\n```\n\n### Request any MIG device\n\nTo request any available MIG device without constraining the profile, use the\n`mig.nvidia.com` DeviceClass with no selectors.\n\n1. Create the `ResourceClaimTemplate`:\n\n   ```yaml\n   apiVersion: resource.k8s.io/v1\n   kind: ResourceClaimTemplate\n   metadata:\n     namespace: mig-example\n     name: any-mig\n   spec:\n     spec:\n       devices:\n         requests:\n         - name: mig\n           exactly:\n             deviceClassName: mig.nvidia.com\n   ```\n\n   Save it as `any-mig.yaml`.\n\n2. Apply the manifest:\n\n   ```bash\n   kubectl apply -f any-mig.yaml\n   ```\n\n3. Create a pod that references the template:\n\n   ```yaml\n   apiVersion: v1\n   kind: Pod\n   metadata:\n     namespace: mig-example\n     name: mig-pod\n   spec:\n     containers:\n     - name: workload\n       image: ubuntu:22.04\n       command: [\"bash\", \"-c\"]\n       args: [\"nvidia-smi -L; sleep 9999\"]\n       resources:\n         claims:\n         - name: mig\n     resourceClaims:\n     - name: mig\n       resourceClaimTemplateName: any-mig\n     tolerations:\n     - key: \"nvidia.com/gpu\"\n       operator: \"Exists\"\n       effect: \"NoSchedule\"\n   ```\n\n   Save it as `mig-pod.yaml`. `ubuntu:22.04` doesn't include `nvidia-smi`;\n   the DRA Driver's CDI integration injects `nvidia-smi` and the host driver's\n   libraries into the container at start, so a plain OS image is sufficient\n   for verification.\n\n4. Apply the manifest:\n\n   ```bash\n   kubectl apply -f mig-pod.yaml\n   ```\n\n5. Verify that the pod received a MIG device:\n\n   ```bash\n   kubectl get pod -n mig-example mig-pod\n   kubectl exec -n mig-example mig-pod -c workload -- nvidia-smi -L\n   ```\n\n### Select a MIG profile\n\nUse a CEL selector to request a specific profile. Profile strings are advertised\nby the driver and vary by GPU model. For CEL selector syntax, refer to\n[Request full GPUs](allocating-gpus.md#select-a-gpu-by-product-name).\n\n```yaml\napiVersion: resource.k8s.io/v1\nkind: ResourceClaimTemplate\nmetadata:\n  namespace: mig-example\n  name: mig-profile\nspec:\n  spec:\n    devices:\n      requests:\n      - name: mig\n        exactly:\n          deviceClassName: mig.nvidia.com\n          selectors:\n          - cel:\n              expression: \"device.attributes['gpu.nvidia.com'].profile == '1g.5gb'\"\n```\n\nSave it as `mig-profile.yaml` and apply with `kubectl apply -f mig-profile.yaml`.\nReference it from a pod with `resourceClaimTemplateName: mig-profile`, following\nthe pod pattern in [Request any MIG device](#request-any-mig-device).\n\n### Request multiple MIG devices from the same GPU\n\nTo ensure that multiple MIG devices in a single claim come from the same\nphysical GPU, add a `constraints` block with\n`matchAttribute: \"gpu.nvidia.com/parentUUID\"`:\n\n```yaml\napiVersion: resource.k8s.io/v1\nkind: ResourceClaimTemplate\nmetadata:\n  namespace: mig-example\n  name: multi-mig\nspec:\n  spec:\n    devices:\n      requests:\n      - name: mig-small\n        exactly:\n          deviceClassName: mig.nvidia.com\n\n---DOC SPLIT---\n---\ntitle: ResourceSlice device attributes\nlinkTitle: ResourceSlice attributes\nweight: 40\ndescription: NVIDIA GPU, MIG, and VFIO device attributes and capacity published by the DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs in ResourceSlices.\n---\n\nThe DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs publishes each allocatable device — full GPUs, MIG\nslices, and VFIO passthrough devices — as an entry under `spec.devices` in a\nnode's `ResourceSlice`. This page is a reference for the NVIDIA-specific\nattributes and capacity on those entries: what each field means and the exact\nnames you reference in CEL selectors.\n\nFor the generic `ResourceSlice` type schema — every field on `ResourceSlice`,\n`Device`, `DeviceAttribute`, `DeviceCapacity`, and related types — see the\n[Kubernetes API reference for ResourceSlice v1](https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/kubernetes-api/resource/resource-slice-v1/).\nFor how the driver publishes slices, see\n[Publishing GPUs in ResourceSlices](../concepts/gpu-allocation.md#publishing-gpus-in-resourceslices).\nTo inspect the ResourceSlices on your own cluster, see\n[View available GPU resources](../guides/gpu-allocation/view-resources.md).\n\n## Device types\n\nThe DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs publishes all devices under a single driver,\n`gpu.nvidia.com`, with one pool per node. The NVIDIA-specific `type` attribute on\neach device identifies the kind of device, and the built-in DeviceClasses select\non it:\n\n| `type` | DeviceClass | Device |\n|---|---|---|\n| `gpu` | `gpu.nvidia.com` | Full physical GPU |\n| `mig` | `mig.nvidia.com` | MIG slice |\n| `vfio` | `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com` | VFIO passthrough device |\n\nThe sections below show a representative `spec.devices[]` entry for each type.\n`kubectl` prints map keys alphabetically (so `type` and `uuid` appear last), the\n`#` comments are annotations rather than part of the real output, and the values\nare illustrative — confirm them on your own cluster.\n\n## Full GPU (type: gpu)\n\n<!-- gpuModuleID with ID, cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/deviceinfo.go:236 -->\n\n```yaml\n- attributes:\n    addressingMode:\n      string: HMM                   # memory addressing mode, when available\n    architecture:\n      string: Ampere                # GPU architecture\n    brand:\n      string: Nvidia                # GPU brand\n    cudaComputeCapability:\n      version: 8.0.0                # CUDA compute capability\n    cudaDriverVersion:\n      version: 13.0.0              # CUDA driver version\n    driverVersion:\n      version: 580.126.20          # NVIDIA driver version\n    gpuModuleID:\n      int: 1                        # Fabric Manager GPU module ID, when enabled\n    partition2:\n      int: 4                        # ID of a reported size-2 FM partition\n    productName:\n      string: NVIDIA A100-PCIE-40GB # product name reported by NVML\n    resource.kubernetes.io/pciBusID:\n      string: 0000:65:00.0          # PCI bus address in BDF notation, when available\n    resource.kubernetes.io/pcieRoot:\n      string: pci0000:64            # PCIe root complex identifier, when available\n    resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode:\n      int: 0                         # NUMA node, when available\n    type:\n      string: gpu                   # device kind: gpu, mig, or vfio\n    uuid:\n      string: GPU-2fa81118-5a5f-aa66-7660-471eed407181\n  capacity:\n    memory:\n      value: 40Gi                   # total GPU memory\n    # On MIG-capable GPUs with partition metadata, additional capacities\n    # (multiprocessors, copyEngines, decoders, encoders, jpegEngines, ofaEngines)\n    # may also appear here.\n  name: gpu-0\n```\n\n## MIG slice (type: mig)\n\n```yaml\n- attributes:\n    addressingMode:\n      string: HMM\n    architecture:\n      string: Ampere                # inherited from the parent GPU\n    brand:\n      string: Nvidia\n    cudaComputeCapability:\n      version: 8.0.0\n    cudaDriverVersion:\n      version: 13.0.0\n    driverVersion:\n      version: 580.126.20\n    parentUUID:\n      string: GPU-2fa81118-5a5f-aa66-7660-471eed407181  # physical GPU hosting this instance\n    productName:\n      string: NVIDIA A100-PCIE-40GB # inherited from the parent GPU\n    profile:\n      string: 1g.5gb                # MIG profile, e.g. 1g.5gb or 3g.20gb\n    resource.kubernetes.io/pciBusID:\n      string: 0000:65:00.0\n    resource.kubernetes.io/pcieRoot:\n      string: pci0000:64\n    resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode:\n      int: 0                         # inherited from the parent GPU, when available\n    type:\n      string: mig                   # device kind\n    uuid:\n      string: MIG-1a2b3c4d-5e6f-7a8b-9c0d-1e2f3a4b5c6d\n  capacity:\n    copyEngines:\n      value: \"1\"                    # dedicated copy engines\n    decoders:\n      value: \"0\"                    # dedicated video decoders\n    encoders:\n      value: \"0\"                    # dedicated video encoders\n    jpegEngines:\n      value: \"0\"                    # dedicated JPEG engines\n    memory:\n      value: 4864Mi                 # dedicated slice memory (usable amount, below the \"5gb\" label)\n    multiprocessors:\n      value: \"14\"                   # streaming multiprocessors dedicated to the slice\n    ofaEngines:\n      value: \"0\"                    # dedicated optical-flow accelerators\n  name: gpu-0-mig-1g.5gb-0\n```\n\n## VFIO passthrough (type: vfio)\n\n```yaml\n- attributes:\n    deviceID:\n      string: \"0x20b0\"              # PCI device ID\n    gpuModuleID:\n      int: 1                         # Fabric Manager GPU module ID, when enabled\n    iommuFDEnabled:\n      bool: true                    # whether the IOMMUFD backend is enabled\n    partition1:\n      int: 8                         # ID of the size-1 Fabric Manager partition\n    partition2:\n      int: 4                         # ID of the size-2 Fabric Manager partition\n    partition4:\n      int: 2                         # ID of the size-4 Fabric Manager partition\n    partition8:\n      int: 1                         # ID of the size-8 Fabric Manager partition\n    productName:\n      string: NVIDIA A100-PCIE-40GB # product name reported by NVML\n    resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode:\n      int: 0                         # NUMA node, when available\n    resource.kubernetes.io/pciBusID:\n      string: 0000:65:00.0          # PCI bus address in BDF notation, when available\n    resource.kubernetes.io/pcieRoot:\n      string: pci0000:64            # PCIe root complex identifier, when available\n    type:\n      string: vfio                  # device kind\n    uuid:\n      string: GPU-2fa81118-5a5f-aa66-7660-471eed407181\n    vendorID:\n      string: \"0x10de\"             # PCI vendor ID (0x10de = NVIDIA)\n  capacity:\n    addressableMemory:\n      value: 40Gi                   # addressable device memory\n  name: gpu-vfio-0\n```\n\n## NUMA locality\n\nThe GPU kubelet plugin publishes the standard\n`resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode` attribute for full GPUs, MIG devices, and\nVFIO devices when the PCI NUMA node is available and non-negative.\n\nBy default, the attribute uses the scalar `int` form shown in the examples.\nWhen you enable both the driver and Kubernetes `DRAListTypeAttributes` feature\ngates, the ResourceSlice serialization changes to a one-element `ints` list:\n\n```yaml\nresource.kubernetes.io/numaNode:\n  ints:\n  - 0\n```\n\nThis representation change matters when inspecting or parsing ResourceSlices,\nbut it does not change the attribute name that you specify in ResourceClaims,\nlike the following example:\n\n```yaml\napiVersion: resource.k8s.io/v1\nkind: ResourceClaimTemplate\nmetadata:\n  name: same-numa-gpus\nspec:\n  spec:\n    devices:\n      requests:\n      - name: gpus\n        exactly:\n          deviceClassName: gpu.nvidia.com\n          allocationMode: ExactCount\n          count: 2\n      constraints:\n      - requests:\n        - gpus\n        matchAttribute: resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode\n```\n\nThis constraint requires all devices selected for `gpus` to have the same\npublished NUMA value. It uses the same\n`resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode` spelling for both the scalar `int` and\none-element `ints` representations.\n\n### Troubleshooting: verify the NUMA value\n\nList each GPU device with its node, PCI bus ID, and published NUMA value:\n\n```bash\n\n---DOC SPLIT---\n---\ntitle: Feature gates\nlinkTitle: Feature gates\nweight: 20\ndescription: Available feature gates, their stages, defaults, and constraints.\n---\n\nFeature gates control experimental and beta functionality in the DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs. They follow [Kubernetes feature gate conventions](https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/command-line-tools-reference/feature-gates/).\n\n## Set feature gates\n\nSet feature gates in your Helm values file:\n\n```yaml\nfeatureGates:\n  TimeSlicingSettings: true\n  MPSSupport: true\n```\n\nOr pass them at install time:\n\n```bash\nhelm install dra-driver-nvidia-gpu oci://registry.k8s.io/dra-driver-nvidia/charts/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu \\\n    --version {{< param \"driver_version\" >}} \\\n    --namespace dra-driver-nvidia-gpu \\\n    --set \"featureGates.TimeSlicingSettings=true\"\n```\n\n## Available feature gates\n\nThe Kubernetes column identifies additional Kubernetes version or Kubernetes feature gate requirements beyond the baseline versions in [Prerequisites](../prerequisites.md).\n\n| Feature gate | Stage | Default | Kubernetes | Description |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| `TimeSlicingSettings` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables customization of CUDA time-slicing settings in `GpuConfig`. |\n| `MPSSupport` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables Multi-Process Service (MPS) sharing strategy in `GpuConfig` and `MigDeviceConfig`. |\n| `IMEXDaemonsWithDNSNames` | Beta | `true` | No requirements. | IMEX daemons use DNS names instead of raw IP addresses for peer communication. See [Kubernetes feature gates](https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/command-line-tools-reference/feature-gates/#DRAPartitionableDevices). | Enables dynamic MIG device allocation and reconfiguration. See [MIG](../guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md). |\n| `NVMLDeviceHealthCheck` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables GPU health checking using NVML. |\n| `ComputeDomainCliques` | Beta | `true` | No requirements. | Uses `ComputeDomainClique` CRD objects to track IMEX daemon membership. Requires `IMEXDaemonsWithDNSNames`. |\n| `CrashOnNVLinkFabricErrors` | Beta | `true` | No requirements. | Causes the kubelet plugin to crash rather than fall back to non-fabric mode when NVLink fabric errors are detected. |\n| `DeviceMetadata` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables IOMMU API device exposure, such as `/dev/iommu` or `/dev/vfio/vfio`, for VFIO workloads with `VfioDeviceConfig`. Each\ninstance's memory and compute are fully separate.\n\nUse MIG when you need predictable, isolated GPU resources for multiple concurrent\nworkloads on the same physical GPU. For how MIG compares to time-slicing and the\nother sharing modes, refer to\n[Choosing a resource type](../../concepts/gpu-allocation.md#choosing-a-resource-type).\n\nEach MIG instance is exposed under the `mig.nvidia.com` DeviceClass and carries\nthe `gpu.nvidia.com/type` attribute value `mig`. Beyond the parent-GPU\nattributes shared with full GPUs, MIG devices add a `profile` (for example\n`1g.5gb`) and a `parentUUID`, and advertise per-slice capacity such as `memory`\nand `multiprocessors`.\n\nFor the full list of MIG attributes and capacity you can match with CEL\nselectors, refer to\n[ResourceSlice device attributes](../../reference/resourceslice-attributes.md#mig-slice-type-mig).\n\nThis guide covers static and dynamic MIG setup and workload examples. For how\nstatic and dynamic MIG work in the driver, refer to\n[Static and dynamic MIG](../../concepts/gpu-allocation.md#static-and-dynamic-mig)\non the GPU allocation concept page. For\nhow to write CEL selectors, refer to\n[Request full GPUs](allocating-gpus.md#select-a-gpu-by-product-name).\n\n## Dynamic MIG feature status\n\n| Feature gate | Default | Stage |\n|---|---|---|\n| `DynamicMIG` | `false` | Alpha |\n\n`DynamicMIG` is mutually exclusive with the following feature gates. The driver\nreturns a validation error at startup if any of these are enabled together:\n\n- `PassthroughSupport`\n- `NVMLDeviceHealthCheck`\n- `MPSSupport`\n\nRefer to the [feature gates reference](../../reference/feature-gates.md) for all\navailable gates.\n\nStatic MIG support is enabled by default; no feature gate is required.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- The DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs must be installed. Refer to [Installation](../../install.md).\n- One or more GPUs in your cluster must support MIG. MIG is available on NVIDIA\n  data center GPUs with the Ampere architecture or newer. For the list of\n  supported GPUs, refer to the\n  [NVIDIA MIG User Guide](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/tesla/mig-user-guide/#supported-gpus).\n- For static MIG, create the MIG partitions on the node before the driver starts.\n  Refer to [Configure static MIG](#configure-static-mig).\n- For dynamic MIG, enable the `DynamicMIG` feature gate. Refer to\n  [Enabling dynamic MIG](#enabling-dynamic-mig).\n\n\n## Enabling dynamic MIG\n\nEnable the `DynamicMIG` feature gate to use dynamic MIG. For how partition\ncreation and teardown work, refer to\n[How dynamic MIG works](../../concepts/gpu-allocation.md#how-dynamic-mig-works).\n\nEnable `DynamicMIG` with `helm upgrade --install`. This installs the driver if\nit is not already present, or upgrades an existing release:\n\n```bash\nhelm upgrade --install dra-driver-nvidia-gpu oci://registry.k8s.io/dra-driver-nvidia/charts/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu \\\n  --namespace dra-driver-nvidia-gpu --create-namespace \\\n  --set featureGates.DynamicMIG=true \\\n  --set gpuResourcesEnabledOverride=true\n```\n\nThe GPU kubelet plugin must restart for the change to take effect. The rolling\nupdate happens automatically when you upgrade the Helm release.\n\nDynamic MIG depends on the Kubernetes partitionable devices feature (KEP-4815).\nOn Kubernetes v1.34–v1.35, you must also enable the `DRAPartitionableDevices`\nfeature gate on the kube-apiserver and kube-scheduler. This feature gate is\nrequired to allow the scheduler to allocate dynamically created MIG devices. On\nKubernetes v1.36 and later, `DRAPartitionableDevices` is enabled by default and\nno action is required.\n\n## Limitations and considerations\n\n- MIG is available only on NVIDIA data center GPUs with the Ampere architecture\n  or newer. Available profiles depend on the GPU model. For supported GPUs and\n  profiles, refer to the\n  [NVIDIA MIG User Guide](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/tesla/mig-user-guide/#supported-gpus).\n- TimeSlicing has no effect on MIG devices. Setting `strategy: TimeSlicing` in a\n  `MigDeviceConfig` does not affect hardware behavior. To share a MIG slice\n  across containers, use Multi-Process Service (MPS) instead with the\n  `MPSSupport` feature gate. Note that `MPSSupport` is mutually exclusive with\n  `DynamicMIG`; they cannot be used together.\n- Static MIG partitions must be configured before the driver starts. In static\n  mode, the driver does not modify the node's MIG configuration. Partitions added\n  after the driver starts are not discovered until the GPU kubelet plugin\n  restarts.\n- Dynamic MIG replaces static discovery. On a MIG-capable node, the driver\n  manages partitions itself and does not use pre-created static partitions.\n  MIG partitions the driver did not create are destroyed when the GPU kubelet\n  plugin starts, so do not enable `DynamicMIG` on nodes with partitions you want\n  to keep.\n- On dynamic MIG nodes, let the driver\n  create and destroy partitions through workload requests. Do not run\n  `mig-parted` or `nvidia-smi mig` while the GPU kubelet plugin is running, because\n  manual changes can conflict with the driver's partition state and cause pod\n  preparation or cleanup to fail.\n- On Ampere GPUs (for example, A100), enable MIG mode on the GPU before the\n  DRA Driver starts. Ampere cannot toggle MIG mode without a GPU reset. If MIG\n  mode is off, the driver falls back to full-GPU allocation and advertises no\n  MIG partitions. Hopper and later architectures enable MIG mode on demand.\n- Dynamic MIG is not supported on vGPU guests.\n\n## Examples\n\nThe following examples work with static or dynamic MIG.\n\n### Create the example namespace\n\nThe examples use the `mig-example` namespace. Create it first:\n\n```bash\nkubectl create namespace mig-example\n```\n\n### Request any MIG device\n\nTo request any available MIG device without constraining the profile, use the\n`mig.nvidia.com` DeviceClass with no selectors.\n\n1. Create the `ResourceClaimTemplate`:\n\n   ```yaml\n   apiVersion: resource.k8s.io/v1\n   kind: ResourceClaimTemplate\n   metadata:\n     namespace: mig-example\n     name: any-mig\n   spec:\n     spec:\n       devices:\n         requests:\n         - name: mig\n           exactly:\n             deviceClassName: mig.nvidia.com\n   ```\n\n   Save it as `any-mig.yaml`.\n\n2. Apply the manifest:\n\n   ```bash\n   kubectl apply -f any-mig.yaml\n   ```\n\n3. Create a pod that references the template:\n\n   ```yaml\n   apiVersion: v1\n   kind: Pod\n   metadata:\n     namespace: mig-example\n     name: mig-pod\n   spec:\n     containers:\n     - name: workload\n       image: ubuntu:22.04\n       command: [\"bash\", \"-c\"]\n       args: [\"nvidia-smi -L; sleep 9999\"]\n       resources:\n         claims:\n         - name: mig\n     resourceClaims:\n     - name: mig\n       resourceClaimTemplateName: any-mig\n     tolerations:\n     - key: \"nvidia.com/gpu\"\n       operator: \"Exists\"\n       effect: \"NoSchedule\"\n   ```\n\n   Save it as `mig-pod.yaml`. `ubuntu:22.04` doesn't include `nvidia-smi`;\n   the DRA Driver's CDI integration injects `nvidia-smi` and the host driver's\n   libraries into the container at start, so a plain OS image is sufficient\n   for verification.\n\n4. Apply the manifest:\n\n   ```bash\n   kubectl apply -f mig-pod.yaml\n   ```\n\n5. Verify that the pod received a MIG device:\n\n   ```bash\n   kubectl get pod -n mig-example mig-pod\n   kubectl exec -n mig-example mig-pod -c workload -- nvidia-smi -L\n   ```\n\n### Select a MIG profile\n\nUse a CEL selector to request a specific profile. Profile strings are advertised\nby the driver and vary by GPU model. For CEL selector syntax, refer to\n[Request full GPUs](allocating-gpus.md#select-a-gpu-by-product-name).\n\n```yaml\napiVersion: resource.k8s.io/v1\nkind: ResourceClaimTemplate\nmetadata:\n  namespace: mig-example\n  name: mig-profile\nspec:\n  spec:\n    devices:\n      requests:\n      - The\n// GIID/CIID-based tracking is however only safe for as long as the very same\n// device is known to be alive (otherwise those IDs may refer to a different\n// device than assumed because they may be re-used for a potentially different\n// physical configuration -- at least, there doesn't seem to be any guarantee\n// that that is not the case). Persist health state or\n\t\t// validate recovery before clearing taints during startup.\n\t\tdriver.wg.Add(1)\n\t\tgo func() {\n\t\t\tdefer driver.wg.Done()\n\t\t\tdriver.deviceHealthEvents(ctx)\n\t\t}()\n\t\tif err := driver.deviceHealthMonitor.Start(ctx); err != nil {\n\t\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"failed to start device health monitor: %w\", err)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"Current kubelet plugin registration status: %s\", helper.RegistrationStatus())\n\n\treturn driver, nil\n}\n\n// GenerateDriverResources() returns the set of DRA ResourceSlices announced by\n// this DRA driver to the system, using the Partitionable Devices paradigm.\nfunc (d *driver) GenerateDriverResources(nodeName string) resourceslice.DriverResources {\n\tif d.useSplitResourceSlices {\n\t\treturn d.generateSplitResourceSlices(nodeName)\n\t}\n\treturn d.generateCombinedResourceSlices(nodeName)\n}\n\n// generateSplitResourceSlices generates ResourceSlices for DynamicMIG for k8s 1.35+.\n// Creates G+1 resource slices for G physical GPUs:\n// - One slice with all SharedCounters (one counter set per GPU).\n// - For each GPU, one slice with devices only (full GPU + MIG partitions).\nfunc (d *driver) generateSplitResourceSlices(nodeName string) resourceslice.DriverResources {\n\tvar gpuslices []resourceslice.Slice\n\tvar allCounterSets []resourceapi.CounterSet\n\n\t// Iterate through `perGPUAllocatable` map in predictable order\n\tfor _, pciBusID := range slices.Sorted(maps.Keys(d.state.perGPUAllocatable.allocatablesMap)) {\n\t\tallocatable := d.state.perGPUAllocatable.allocatablesMap[pciBusID]\n\t\tvar deviceSlice resourceslice.Slice\n\t\tvar gpuInfo *GpuInfo\n\n\t\t// Stable sort order by devicename\n\t\tfor _, devname := range slices.Sorted(maps.Keys(allocatable)) {\n\t\t\tdevice := allocatable[devname]\n\t\t\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"About to announce device %s\", devname)\n\n\t\t\t// Remember this GPU so we can emit exactly one shared counter\n\t\t\t// set for it below. MIG partitions reference the parent GPU's\n\t\t\t// counter set by name, so the counter set must be emitted even\n\t\t\t// when the full GPU itself is not announced (e.g. on Ampere\n\t\t\t// with MIG mode enabled, where MIG cannot be toggled without a\n\t\t\t// GPU reset and so only MIG partitions are allocatable).\n\t\t\tif gpuInfo == nil {\n\t\t\t\tswitch {\n\t\t\t\tcase device.Gpu != nil:\n\t\t\t\t\tgpuInfo = device.Gpu\n\t\t\t\tcase device.MigDynamic != nil:\n\t\t\t\t\tgpuInfo = device.MigDynamic.Parent\n\t\t\t\t}\n\t\t\t}\n\n\t\t\t// Add device/partition to the device-only slice for this GPU.\n\t\t\tdeviceSlice.Devices = append(deviceSlice.Devices, device.PartGetDevice(d.state.config))\n\t\t}\n\t\tif gpuInfo != nil {\n\t\t\tallCounterSets = append(allCounterSets, gpuInfo.PartSharedCounterSets()...)\n\t\t}\n\n---\n\t\t}\n\t\tgpuslices = append(gpuslices, deviceSlice)\n\t}\n\n\tsharedCountersSlice := resourceslice.Slice{\n\t\tSharedCounters: allCounterSets,\n\t}\n\n\t// Emit the `sharedCountersSlice` first.\n\tgpuslices = append([]resourceslice.Slice{sharedCountersSlice}, gpuslices...)\n\n\treturn resourceslice.DriverResources{\n\t\tPools: map[string]resourceslice.Pool{\n\t\t\tnodeName: {Slices: gpuslices},\n\t\t},\n\t}\n}\n\n// generateCombinedResourceSlices generates ResourceSlices for DynamicMIG for k8s 1.34.\n// Creates G resource slices for G physical GPUs, each containing both,\n// SharedCounters and Devices.\nfunc (d *driver) generateCombinedResourceSlices(nodeName string) resourceslice.DriverResources {\n\tvar gpuslices []resourceslice.Slice\n\n\t// Iterate through `perGPUAllocatable` map in predictable order so that the\n\t// slices get published in predictable order.\n\tfor _, pciBusID := range slices.Sorted(maps.Keys(d.state.perGPUAllocatable.allocatablesMap)) {\n\t\tallocatable := d.state.perGPUAllocatable.allocatablesMap[pciBusID]\n\t\tvar slice resourceslice.Slice\n\t\tvar gpuInfo *GpuInfo\n\n\t\t// Stable sort order by devicename -- makes the order of devices\n\t\t// presented in a resource slice reproducible. Good for debuggability /\n\t\t// readability, and leads to a minimal slice diff during kubelet plugin\n\t\t// restart (the slice diff is logged).\n\t\tfor _, devname := range slices.Sorted(maps.Keys(allocatable)) {\n\t\t\tdevice := allocatable[devname]\n\t\t\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"About to announce device %s\", devname)\n\n\t\t\t// Remember this GPU so we can emit exactly one shared counter\n\t\t\t// set for it below. MIG partitions reference the parent GPU's\n\t\t\t// counter set by name, so the counter set must be emitted even\n\t\t\t// when the full GPU itself is not announced (e.g. on Ampere\n\t\t\t// with MIG mode enabled, where MIG cannot be toggled without a\n\t\t\t// GPU reset and so only MIG partitions are allocatable).\n\t\t\tif gpuInfo == nil {\n\t\t\t\tswitch {\n\t\t\t\tcase device.Gpu != nil:\n\t\t\t\t\tgpuInfo = device.Gpu\n\t\t\t\tcase device.MigDynamic != nil:\n\t\t\t\t\tgpuInfo = device.MigDynamic.Parent\n\t\t\t\t}\n\t\t\t}\n\n\t\t\t// Add all allocatable devices for this physical GPU to this slice.\n\t\t\t// This includes not-yet-manifested MIG devices, and the physical\n\t\t\t// GPU itself.\n\t\t\tslice.Devices = append(slice.Devices, device.PartGetDevice(d.state.config))\n\t\t}\n\n\t\tif gpuInfo != nil {\n\t\t\tslice.SharedCounters = gpuInfo.PartSharedCounterSets()\n\t\t}\n\t\tgpuslices = append(gpuslices, slice)\n\t}\n\n\treturn resourceslice.DriverResources{\n\t\tPools: map[string]resourceslice.Pool{\n\t\t\tnodeName: {Slices: gpuslices},\n\t\t},\n\t}\n}\n\nfunc (d *driver) Shutdown() error {\n\tif d == nil {\n\t\treturn nil\n\t}\n\n\tif d.healthcheck != nil {\n\t\td.healthcheck.Stop()\n\t}\n\n\t// Shut down long-lived NVML session.\n\tif featuregates.Enabled(featuregates.DynamicMIG) {\n\t\td.state.nvdevlib.alwaysShutdown()\n\t}\n\n\t// Tear down the Fabric Manager connection, if one was opened.\n\tif d.state.fmManager != nil {\n\t\tif err := d.state.fmManager.Close(); err != nil {\n\t\t\tklog.Warningf(\"error closing Fabric Manager connection: %v\", err)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tif d.deviceHealthMonitor != nil {\n\t\td.deviceHealthMonitor.Stop()\n\t}\n\n\td.wg.Wait()\n\n\tif err := d.state.checkpointCleanupManager.Stop(); err != nil {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"error stopping CheckpointCleanupManager: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\n\td.pluginhelper.Stop()\n\treturn nil\n}\n\nfunc (d *driver) PrepareResourceClaims(ctx context.Context, claims []*resourceapi.ResourceClaim) (map[types.UID]kubeletplugin.PrepareResult, error) {\n\n\tif len(claims) == 0 {\n\t\t// That's probably the health check, log that on higher verbosity level\n\t\tklog.V(7).Infof(\"PrepareResourceClaims called with %d claim(s)\", len(claims))\n\t} else {\n\t\t// Log canonical string representation for each claim injected here --\n\t\t// we've noticed that this can greatly facilitate debugging.\n\t\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"Prepare called for: %v\", ClaimsToStrings(claims))\n\t}\n\n\tresults := make(map[types.UID]kubeletplugin.PrepareResult)\n\n\tfor _, claim := range claims {\n\t\tresults[claim.UID] = d.nodePrepareResource(ctx, claim)\n\t}\n\n\treturn results, nil\n}\n\nfunc (d *driver) UnprepareResourceClaims(ctx context.Context, claimRefs []kubeletplugin.NamespacedObject) (map[types.UID]error, error) {\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"Unprepare called for: %v\", ClaimRefsToStrings(claimRefs))\n\tresults := make(map[types.UID]error)\n\tfor _, claimRef := range claimRefs {\n\t\tresults[claimRef.UID] = d.nodeUnprepareResource(ctx, claimRef)\n\t}\n\n\treturn results, nil\n}\n\nfunc (d *driver) HandleError(ctx context.Context, err error, msg string) {\n\t// For now we just follow the advice documented in the DRAPlugin API docs.\n\t// See: https://pkg.go.dev/k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/util/runtime#HandleErrorWithContext\n\truntime.HandleErrorWithContext(ctx, err, msg)\n}\n\nfunc (d *driver) WatchHealthStatus(context.Context, chan<- kubeletplugin.DeviceHealthReport) error {\n\treturn kubeletplugin.ErrHealthNotSupported\n}\n\nfunc (d *driver) nodePrepareResource(ctx context.Context, claim *resourceapi.ResourceClaim) kubeletplugin.PrepareResult {\n\tt0 := time.Now()\n\t// Instead of a global prepare/unprepare (PU) lock, we could rely on\n\t// fine-grained checkpoint locking, which was proven to work correctly in\n\t// case of DynamicMIG mode. However, out of caution, retain this global PU\n\t// lock for now in all modes (re-evaluate the performance impact at a later\n\t// time).\n\n\trelease, err := d.pulock.Acquire(ctx, flock.WithTimeout(10*time.Second))\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tdrametrics.IncNodePrepareError(DriverName, \"lock_acquire\")\n\t\treturn kubeletplugin.PrepareResult{\n\t\t\tErr: fmt.Errorf(\"error acquiring prep/unprep lock: %w\", err),\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\tdefer release()\n\tdoneInFlight := drametrics.TrackInFlight(DriverName, \"prepare\")\n\tdefer doneInFlight()\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"t_prep_lock_acq %.3f s\", time.Since(t0).Seconds())\n\n\tcs := ResourceClaimToString(claim)\n\ttprep0 := time.Now()\n\tdevs, err := d.state.Prepare(ctx, claim)\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"t_prep %.3f s (claim %s)\", time.Since(tprep0).Seconds(), cs)\n\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tdrametrics.IncNodePrepareError(DriverName, \"prepare_devices\")\n\t\treturn kubeletplugin.PrepareResult{\n\t\t\tErr: fmt.Errorf(\"error preparing devices for claim %s: %w\", cs, err),\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tif featuregates.Enabled(featuregates.PassthroughSupport) {\n\t\t// Re-advertise updated resourceslice after preparing devices.\n\t\tif err = d.publishResources(ctx, d.state.config); err != nil {\n\t\t\tdrametrics.IncNodePrepareError(DriverName, \"publish_resources\")\n\t\t\treturn kubeletplugin.PrepareResult{\n\t\t\t\tErr: fmt.Errorf(\"error preparing devices for claim %v: %w\", claim.UID, err),\n\t\t\t}\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tklog.Infof(\"Returning newly prepared devices for claim '%s': %v\", cs, devs)\n\tdrametrics.ObserveRequest(DriverName, \"prepare\", time.Since(t0))\n\treturn kubeletplugin.PrepareResult{Devices: devs}\n}\n\nfunc (d *driver) nodeUnprepareResource(ctx context.Context, claimRef kubeletplugin.NamespacedObject) error {\n\tt0 := time.Now()\n\n\trelease, err := d.pulock.Acquire(ctx, flock.WithTimeout(10*time.Second))\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tdrametrics.IncNodeUnprepareError(DriverName, \"lock_acquire\")\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"error acquiring prep/unprep lock: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\tdefer release()\n\tdoneInFlight := drametrics.TrackInFlight(DriverName, \"unprepare\")\n\tdefer doneInFlight()\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"t_unprep_lock_acq %.3f s\", time.Since(t0).Seconds())\n\n\tcs := claimRef.String()\n\ttunprep0 := time.Now()\n\ttaintRemovedRepublish, err := d.state.Unprepare(ctx, claimRef)\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"t_unprep %.3f s (claim %s)\", time.Since(tunprep0).Seconds(), cs)\n\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tdrametrics.IncNodeUnprepareError(DriverName, \"unprepare_devices\")\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"error unpreparing devices for claim %v: %w\", claimRef.String(), err)\n\t}\n\n\tif featuregates.Enabled(featuregates.PassthroughSupport) ||\n\t\t(featuregates.Enabled(featuregates.DynamicMIG) &&\n\t\t\tfeaturegates.Enabled(featuregates.NVMLDeviceHealthCheck) &&\n\t\t\ttaintRemovedRepublish) {\n\t\t// Re-advertise updated resourceslice after unpreparing devices\n\t\t// or removed a Dynamic MIG XID taint.\n\t\tif err = d.publishResources(ctx, d.state.config); err != nil {\n\t\t\tdrametrics.IncNodeUnprepareError(DriverName, \"publish_resources\")\n\t\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"error publishing resources: %w\", err)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tdrametrics.ObserveRequest(DriverName, \"unprepare\", time.Since(t0))\n\treturn nil\n}\n\nfunc (d *driver) publishResources(ctx context.Context, config *Config) error {\n\n\tif featuregates.Enabled(featuregates.DynamicMIG) {\n\t\t// From KEP 4815: \"we will add client-side validation in the\n\t\t// ResourceSlice controller helper, so that any errors in the\n\t\t// ResourceSlices will be caught before they even are applied to the\n\t\t// APIServer\" -- the helper below is being referred to.\n\t\t//\n\t\t// TODO: implement error handler for bad slices:\n\t\t// https://github.com/kubernetes/kubernetes/commit/a171795e313ee9f407fef4897c1a1e2052120991\n\t\tklog.V(1).Infof(\"featuregates.DynamicMIG enabled: construct ResourceSlice objects according to KEP 4815 (partitionable devices)\")\n\t\tresources := d.GenerateDriverResources(config.flags.nodeName)\n\t\tif err := d.pluginhelper.PublishResources(ctx, resources); err != nil {\n\t\t\treturn err\n\t\t}\n\t\treturn nil\n\t}\n\n\t// Enumerate the set of GPU, MIG and VFIO devices and publish them\n\tvar resourceSlice resourceslice.Slice\n\tfor _, devices := range d.state.perGPUAllocatable.allocatablesMap {\n\t\tfor _, device := range devices {\n\t\t\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"About to announce device %s\", device.GetDevice(config).Name)\n\t\t\tresourceSlice.Devices = append(resourceSlice.Devices, device.GetDevice(config))\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\n---\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"t_prep_state_lock_acq %.3f s\", time.Since(tplock0).Seconds())\n\n\tclaimUID := string(claim.UID)\n\n\ttgcp0 := time.Now()\n\tcp, err := s.getCheckpoint(ctx)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"unable to get checkpoint: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\tklog.V(7).Infof(\"t_prep_get_checkpoint %.3f s\", time.Since(tgcp0).Seconds())\n\n\t// Check for existing 'completed' claim preparation before updating the\n\t// checkpoint with 'PrepareStarted'. Otherwise, we effectively mark a\n\t// perfectly prepared claim as only partially prepared, which may have\n\t// negative side effects during Unprepare() (currently a noop in this case:\n\t// unprepare noop: claim preparation started but not completed).\n\tpreparedClaim, exists := cp.V2.PreparedClaims[claimUID]\n\tif exists && preparedClaim.CheckpointState == ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareCompleted {\n\t\t// Make this a noop. Associated device(s) has/ave been prepared by us.\n\t\t// Prepare() must be idempotent, as it may be invoked more than once per\n\t\t// claim (and actual device preparation must happen at most once).\n\t\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"Skip prepare: claim already in PrepareCompleted state: %s\", ResourceClaimToString(claim))\n\t\treturn preparedClaim.PreparedDevices.GetDevices(), nil\n\t}\n\n\t// In certain scenarios, the same device can be prepared/allocated more than once for different claims\n\t// due to races between data processing in different goroutines in the scheduler, or when pods are\n\t// force-deleted while the kubelet still considers the devices allocated.\n\t// To prevent this, we check whether any device requested in the incoming claim has already been prepared\n\t// and fail the request if so (unless the prior preparation was performed with admin access).\n\t// More details: https://github.com/kubernetes/kubernetes/pull/136269\n\tif err := s.validateNoOverlappingPreparedDevices(cp, claim); err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"unable to prepare claim %v: %w\", claimUID, err)\n\t}\n\n\t// Relevant for DynamicMIG: a previous preparation attempt for the same\n\t// claim might have resulted in complete or partial GI/CI creation. Roll\n\t// that back, and retry creation from scratch (that maybe can later be\n\t// optimized into filling the gaps).\n\tif exists && preparedClaim.CheckpointState == ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareStarted {\n\t\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"Claim %s already in PrepareStarted state: attempt rollback before new prepare\", ResourceClaimToString(claim))\n\t\tif err := s.rollbackPartiallyPreparedClaim(ctx, claimUID, preparedClaim, cp); err != nil {\n\t\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"rollback failed for partially prepared claim %s failed: %w\", PreparedClaimToString(&preparedClaim, claimUID), err)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\ttucp0 := time.Now()\n\terr = s.updateCheckpoint(ctx, func(cp *Checkpoint) {\n\t\tcp.V2.PreparedClaims[claimUID] = PreparedClaim{\n\t\t\tCheckpointState: ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareStarted,\n\t\t\tStatus:          claim.Status,\n\t\t\tName:            claim.Name,\n\t\t\tNamespace:       claim.Namespace,\n\t\t}\n\t})\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"unable to update checkpoint: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"t_prep_update_checkpoint %.3f s\", time.Since(tucp0).Seconds())\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"checkpoint updated for claim %v\", claimUID)\n\n\ttprep0 := time.Now()\n\tpreparedDevices, err := s.prepareDevices(ctx, claim, cp)\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"t_prep_core %.3f s (claim %s)\", time.Since(tprep0).Seconds(), ResourceClaimToString(claim))\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"prepare devices failed: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\n\t// TODO: Remove this once partitionable device support is introduced for vfio devices.\n\tif featuregates.Enabled(featuregates.PassthroughSupport) {\n\t\tfor _, device := range preparedDevices.GetDevices() {\n\t\t\tallocatableDevice := s.perGPUAllocatable.GetAllocatableDevice(device.DeviceName)\n\t\t\tif allocatableDevice == nil {\n\t\t\t\tklog.Warningf(\"allocatable not found for device: %v\", device.DeviceName)\n\t\t\t\tcontinue\n\t\t\t}\n\t\t\t// Remove all other device types on the parent GPU of the prepared device.\n\t\t\t// When vfio type is prepared, gpu type should not be advertised and vice versa.\n\t\t\ts.perGPUAllocatable.RemoveSiblingDevices(allocatableDevice)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\ttccsf0 := time.Now()\n\tif err := s.cdi.CreateClaimSpecFile(claimUID, preparedDevices); err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"unable to create CDI spec file for claim: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\tklog.V(7).Infof(\"t_prep_ccsf %.3f s\", time.Since(tccsf0).Seconds())\n\n\ttucp20 := time.Now()\n\terr = s.updateCheckpoint(ctx, func(cp *Checkpoint) {\n\t\tcp.V2.PreparedClaims[claimUID] = PreparedClaim{\n\t\t\tCheckpointState: ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareCompleted,\n\t\t\tStatus:          claim.Status,\n\t\t\tPreparedDevices: preparedDevices,\n\t\t}\n\t})\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn nil, fmt.Errorf(\"unable to update checkpoint: %w\", err)\n\t}\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"checkpoint updated for claim %v\", claimUID)\n\tklog.V(7).Infof(\"t_prep_ucp2 %.3f s\", time.Since(tucp20).Seconds())\n\n\treturn preparedDevices.GetDevices(), nil\n}\n\n// DestroyUnknownMIGDevices() relies on the checkpoint as the source of truth.\n// It tries to tear down any existing MIG device that is not referenced by\n// currently checkpointed claims in ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareCompleted state.\n//\n// This routine is currently called once during startup before accepting\n// requests from the kubelet. TODO: a better mechanism to make sure it does not\n// conflict with overlapping Prepare() and Unprepare() activities (from e.g. a\n// separate plugin process, during an upgrade) would be to hold on to file-based\n// lock on the checkpoint (during the entire operation). Notworthy: the\n// operation may potentially take significant wall time.\n//\n// We need to learn over time how workload running on a to-be-torn-down MIG\n// device may and should affect the teardown attempt.\n//\n// There may be a fundamental need to invoke this type of cleanup periodically,\n// instead of once during startup.\n//\n// Note: there are other cleanup strategies performed at runtime: (1) periodic\n// cleanup of partially prepared but _stale_ claims (2) attempted rollback in\n// the Prepare() path for a specific, non-stale claim). Especially once/if this\n// method here is executed periodically, there is overlap with (1) -- but there\n// are decisive differences:\n//\n// - This routine here (0) does not mutate the checkpoint. It only tears down\n// devices. (1) removes entries from the checkpoint after confirming cleanup.\n// (1) may also tear down MIG devices that (0) would otherwise tear down: one\n// wins, and that is OK.\n//\n// - (2) is something that (1) cannot achieve, because the claim in question is\n// not stale. A previous Prepare() attempt was executed partially and any state\n// mutations performed may make an immediately retried Prepare() fail (with\n// \"insufficient resources\"). In that case, (2) performs an active rollback in\n// the business logic of the retried Prepare() call, to help resolve the\n// conflict quickly (from the user's point of view). (0) may also help but not\n// in a timely fashion.\n//\n// All cleanup strategies are critical but also dangerous -- when designed or\n// implemented wrongly, they may affect workload.\n//\n// Significance of this cleanup:\n//\n// 1) Healing out-of-band MIG device creation. Forbidden by design (make sure to\n// document tha). Sometimes, MIG devices however still get created manually,\n// out-of-band). It is convenient for the system to recover from that\n// automatically.\n//\n// 2) Recovering from interruptions within a multi-step transaction (as of bugs\n// within this plugin, issues in the NVML management layer, invasive operations,\n// etc). There is a lot of room, especially over time, for state to be drifting\n// as of partially performed transactions.\nfunc (s *DeviceState) DestroyUnknownMIGDevices(ctx context.Context) {\n\tlogpfx := \"Destroy unknown MIG devices\"\n\tcp, err := s.getCheckpoint(ctx)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tklog.Errorf(\"%s: unable to get checkpoint: %s\", logpfx, err)\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\t// Get checkpointed claims in PrepareCompleted state. Only MIG devices\n\t// referenced in those will be retained. That is, the below's logic tears\n\t// down MIG devices that correspond to claims in a PrepareStarted limbo\n\t// state -- that can only be correct when there are no overlapping\n\t// NodePrepareResources() executions.\n\tfiltered := make(PreparedClaimsByUIDV2)\n\tfor uid, claim := range cp.V2.PreparedClaims {\n\t\tif claim.CheckpointState == ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareCompleted {\n\t\t\tfiltered[uid] = claim\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tvar expectedDeviceNames []DeviceName\n\tfor _, cpclaim := range filtered {\n\t\tfor _, res := range cpclaim.Status.Allocation.Devices.Results {\n\t\t\texpectedDeviceNames = append(expectedDeviceNames, res.Device)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tklog.Infof(\"%s: enter teardown routine (%d expect devices: %s)\", logpfx, len(expectedDeviceNames), expectedDeviceNames)\n\n\t// For now, let this be best-effort. Upon error, proceed with the program,\n\t// do not crash it. TODO: a better mechanism to make sure it does not\n// conflict with overlapping Prepare() and Unprepare() activities (from e.g. a\n// separate plugin process, during an upgrade) would be to hold on to file-based\n// lock on the checkpoint (during the entire operation). Notworthy: the\n// operation may potentially take significant wall time.\n//\n// We need to learn over time how workload running on a to-be-torn-down MIG\n// device may and should affect the teardown attempt.\n//\n// There may be a fundamental need to invoke this type of cleanup periodically,\n// instead of once during startup.\n//\n// Note: there are other cleanup strategies performed at runtime: (1) periodic\n// cleanup of partially prepared but _stale_ claims (2) attempted rollback in\n// the Prepare() path for a specific, non-stale claim). Especially once/if this\n// method here is executed periodically, there is overlap with (1) -- but there\n// are decisive differences:\n//\n// - This routine here (0) does not mutate the checkpoint. It only tears down\n// devices. (1) removes entries from the checkpoint after confirming cleanup.\n// (1) may also tear down MIG devices that (0) would otherwise tear down: one\n// wins, and that is OK.\n//\n// - (2) is something that (1) cannot achieve, because the claim in question is\n// not stale. A previous Prepare() attempt was executed partially and any state\n// mutations performed may make an immediately retried Prepare() fail (with\n// \"insufficient resources\"). In that case, (2) performs an active rollback in\n// the business logic of the retried Prepare() call, to help resolve the\n// conflict quickly (from the user's point of view). (0) may also help but not\n// in a timely fashion.\n//\n// All cleanup strategies are critical but also dangerous -- when designed or\n// implemented wrongly, they may affect workload.\n//\n// Significance of this cleanup:\n//\n// 1) Healing out-of-band MIG device creation. Forbidden by design (make sure to\n// document tha). Sometimes, MIG devices however still get created manually,\n// out-of-band). It is convenient for the system to recover from that\n// automatically.\n//\n// 2) Recovering from interruptions within a multi-step transaction (as of bugs\n// within this plugin, issues in the NVML management layer, invasive operations,\n// etc). There is a lot of room, especially over time, for state to be drifting\n// as of partially performed transactions.\nfunc (s *DeviceState) DestroyUnknownMIGDevices(ctx context.Context) {\n\tlogpfx := \"Destroy unknown MIG devices\"\n\tcp, err := s.getCheckpoint(ctx)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tklog.Errorf(\"%s: unable to get checkpoint: %s\", logpfx, err)\n\t\treturn\n\n---\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\t// Get checkpointed claims in PrepareCompleted state. Only MIG devices\n\t// referenced in those will be retained. That is, the below's logic tears\n\t// down MIG devices that correspond to claims in a PrepareStarted limbo\n\t// state -- that can only be correct when there are no overlapping\n\t// NodePrepareResources() executions.\n\tfiltered := make(PreparedClaimsByUIDV2)\n\tfor uid, claim := range cp.V2.PreparedClaims {\n\t\tif claim.CheckpointState == ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareCompleted {\n\t\t\tfiltered[uid] = claim\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tvar expectedDeviceNames []DeviceName\n\tfor _, cpclaim := range filtered {\n\t\tfor _, res := range cpclaim.Status.Allocation.Devices.Results {\n\t\t\texpectedDeviceNames = append(expectedDeviceNames, res.Device)\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tklog.Infof(\"%s: enter teardown routine (%d expect devices: %s)\", logpfx, len(expectedDeviceNames), expectedDeviceNames)\n\n\t// For now, let this be best-effort. TypeUnprepCallable = func(ctx context.Context, claimRef kubeletplugin.NamespacedObject) error\n\ntype CheckpointCleanupManager struct {\n\twaitGroup     sync.WaitGroup\n\tcancelContext
