*DeviceState, client *draclient.Client) *CheckpointCleanupManager {\n\t// `queue`: buffered channel to implement a pragmatic fixed-size queue; to\n\t// ensure at most one cleanup operation gets enqueued. TODO: review\n\t// replacing this with a condition variable.\n\treturn &CheckpointCleanupManager{\n\t\tdevicestate: s,\n\t\tdraclient:   client,\n\t\tqueue:       make(chan struct{}, 1),\n\t}\n}\n\nfunc (m *CheckpointCleanupManager) Start(ctx context.Context, unprepfunc TypeUnprepCallable) error {\n\tctx, cancel := context.WithCancel(ctx)\n\tm.cancelContext = cancel\n\tm.unprepfunc = unprepfunc\n\n\tm.waitGroup.Add(1)\n\tgo func() {\n\t\tdefer m.waitGroup.Done()\n\t\t// Start producer: periodically submit cleanup task.\n\t\tm.triggerPeriodically(ctx)\n\t}()\n\n\tm.waitGroup.Add(1)\n\tgo func() {\n\t\tdefer m.waitGroup.Done()\n\t\t// Start consumer\n\t\tm.worker(ctx)\n\t}()\n\n\tklog.V(6).Infof(\"CheckpointCleanupManager started\")\n\treturn nil\n}\n\nfunc (m *CheckpointCleanupManager) Stop() error {\n\tif m.cancelContext != nil {\n\t\tm.cancelContext()\n\t}\n\tm.waitGroup.Wait()\n\treturn nil\n}\n\n// cleanup() is the high-level cleanup routine run once upon plugin startup and\n// then periodically. It gets all claims in PrepareStarted state from the\n// current checkpoint, and runs `unprepareIfStale()` for each of them. Each\n// invocation of `cleanup()` and each invocation of `unprepareIfStale()` is\n// best-effort: errors do not need to be propagated (but are expected to be\n// properly logged).\n//\n// Note: This function does not acquire DeviceState lock when reading the checkpoint.\n// The lock is not needed because:\n//  1. This initial checkpoint read is just discovering candidate claims to check - it\n//     doesn't need strong consistency. Missing a claim due to concurrent write just\n//     means we catch it on the next periodic iteration.\n//  2. We validate each candidate against the API server (the authoritative source of\n//     truth for whether a claim is truly stale), not the local checkpoint state.\n//  3. The actual checkpoint mutation happens in nodeUnprepareResource(), which properly\n//     acquires the pulock (process-level file lock) for atomic read-modify-write.\n//  4. Holding DeviceState lock during the entire cleanup (which could take seconds with\n//     multiple API calls) would unnecessarily block normal Prepare/Unprepare operations.\nfunc (m *CheckpointCleanupManager) cleanup(ctx context.Context) {\n\tcp, err := m.devicestate.getCheckpoint(ctx)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tklog.Errorf(\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: unable to get checkpoint: %s\", err)\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\t// Get checkpointed claims in PrepareStarted state.\n\tfiltered := make(PreparedClaimsByUIDV2)\n\tfor uid, claim := range cp.V2.PreparedClaims {\n\t\tif claim.CheckpointState == ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareStarted {\n\t\t\tfiltered[uid] = claim\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\n\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: claims in PrepareStarted state: %d (of %d)\", len(filtered), len(cp.V2.PreparedClaims))\n\n\tfor cpuid, cpclaim := range filtered {\n\t\tm.unprepareIfStale(ctx, cpuid, cpclaim)\n\t}\n}\n\n// Detect if claim is stale (not known to the API server). Call unprepare() if\n// it is stale.\n//\n// There are two options to look up a claim with a specific UID from the API\n// server:\n//\n//  1. List(), with `FieldSelector: \"metadata.uid=<uid>\"`. Especially\n//     across all namespaces (but also within one namespace) this is\n//     irresponsibly expensive lookup, cannot be done on a routine\n//     basis.\n//\n//  2. Get(), using a specific name/namespace + subsequent UID\n//     comparison. This is a cheap lookup for the API server.\n//\n// For (2), name and namespace must be stored in the checkpoint. That is not\n// true for legacy deployments with checkpoint data created by version 25.3.x of\n// this driver. Detect that situation by looking for an empty `Name`.\nfunc (m *CheckpointCleanupManager) unprepareIfStale(ctx context.Context, cpuid string, cpclaim PreparedClaim) {\n\tif cpclaim.Name == \"\" {\n\t\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: skip checkpointed claim '%s': RC name not in checkpoint\", cpuid)\n\t\t// Consider fallback: expensive lookup by UID across all namespaces.\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tclaim, err := m.getClaimByName(ctx, cpclaim.Name, cpclaim.Namespace)\n\tif err != nil && errors.IsNotFound(err) {\n\t\tklog.V(4).Infof(\n\t\t\t\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: partially prepared claim '%s/%s:%s' is stale: not found in API server\",\n\t\t\tcpclaim.Namespace,\n\t\t\tcpclaim.Name,\n\t\t\tcpuid)\n\t\tm.unprepare(ctx, cpuid, cpclaim)\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\t// A transient error during API server lookup. No explicit retry required.\n\t// The next periodic cleanup invocation will implicitly retry.\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tklog.Infof(\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: skip for checkpointed claim %s: getClaimByName failed (retry later): %s\", cpuid, err)\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tif string(claim.UID) != cpuid {\n\t\t// There cannot be two ResourceClaim objects with the same name in\n\t\t// the same namespace at the same time. It is possible for a\n\t\t// ResourceClaim with the same name to have a different UID if the\n\t\t// original object was deleted and a new one with the same name was\n\t\t// created. Hence, this checkpointed claim is stale.\n\t\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: partially prepared claim '%s/%s' is stale: UID changed (checkpoint: %s, API server: %s)\", cpclaim.Namespace, cpclaim.Name, cpuid, claim.UID)\n\t\tm.unprepare(ctx, cpuid, cpclaim)\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tklog.V(4).Infof(\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: partially prepared claim not stale: %s\", ResourceClaimToString(claim))\n}\n\n// unprepare() attempts to unprepare devices for the provided claim\n// ('self-initiated unprepare'). Expected side effect: removal of the\n// corresponding claim from the checkpoint.\nfunc (m *CheckpointCleanupManager) unprepare(ctx context.Context, uid string, claim PreparedClaim) {\n\tclaimRef := kubeletplugin.NamespacedObject{\n\t\tUID: types.UID(uid),\n\t\tNamespacedName: types.NamespacedName{\n\t\t\tName:      claim.Name,\n\t\t\tNamespace: claim.Namespace,\n\t\t},\n\t}\n\n\t// Perform one Unprepare attempt. Implicit retrying across periodic cleanup\n\t// invocations is sufficient. Rely on Unprepare() to delete claim from\n\t// checkpoint (upon success). TODO: review `Unprepare()` for code paths that\n\t// allow for this claim never to be dropped from the checkpoint (resulting\n\t// in infinite periodic cleanup attempts for this claim).\n\terr := m.unprepfunc(ctx, claimRef)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tklog.Warningf(\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: error during unprepare for %s (retried later): %s\", claimRef.String(), err)\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\n\tklog.Infof(\"Checkpointed RC cleanup: unprepared stale claim: %s\", claimRef.String())\n}\n\n// getClaimByName() attempts to fetch a ResourceClaim object directly from the\n// API server.\nfunc (m *CheckpointCleanupManager) getClaimByName(ctx context.Context, name string, ns string) (*resourcev1.ResourceClaim, error) {\n\t// The API call below should be responded to with low latency. {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_24","type":"agent_message","text":"Used `dra-gpu-allocation-workflows` and traced the current repo’s documented + implemented path for a **dynamic MIG** claim.\n\n**End-to-End Flow**\n- A pod references a `ResourceClaim` or `ResourceClaimTemplate`; the request uses `deviceClassName: mig.nvidia.com`, optionally with CEL selectors like `device.attributes['gpu.nvidia.com'].profile == '1g.5gb'` to choose a MIG profile. See `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md:113` and `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md:170`.\n- `mig.nvidia.com` maps to devices whose published `gpu.nvidia.com/type` is `mig`; MIG devices also expose attributes like `profile` and `parentUUID`, which selectors and constraints can target. See `site/content/docs/reference/resourceslice-attributes.md:26` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/mig.go:139`.\n- For dynamic MIG, the driver must run with `DynamicMIG=true`; on Kubernetes `v1.34`–`v1.35`, the cluster also needs `DRAPartitionableDevices=true`, while `v1.36+` enables that by default. `DynamicMIG` is mutually exclusive with `PassthroughSupport` and `MPSSupport`. See `site/content/docs/reference/feature-gates.md:39`, `site/content/docs/reference/feature-gates.md:55`, and `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md:31`.\n\n**Selection and Scheduling**\n- On startup, dynamic MIG does **not** publish only existing slices. Instead, it advertises every valid abstract MIG partition placement on each MIG-capable GPU plus shared counters that represent overlapping capacity constraints. That is the scheduler-facing inventory model. See `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:76`.\n- The kubelet plugin switches to the partitionable-device `ResourceSlice` model when `DynamicMIG` is enabled, via `publishResources()`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/driver.go:478`.\n- The scheduler matches the claim’s `DeviceClass`, selectors, and any constraints against those published `ResourceSlices`, then records the allocation on a node. The crucial dynamic-MIG nuance is: the allocated device can be an **abstract future MIG slice**, not a pre-existing hardware instance. That behavior is documented in `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:76`.\n\n**Claim Config Handling**\n- Any opaque NVIDIA config attached to the claim for MIG is parsed as `MigDeviceConfig`, which currently mainly carries sharing settings. See `api/nvidia.com/resource/v1beta1/migconfig.go:26`.\n- During prepare, the plugin groups allocation results by attached config, normalizes the config, validates it, and applies it before assembling prepared devices. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:991`.\n- `MigDeviceConfig.Normalize()` and `Validate()` are the gatekeepers for implied defaults and sharing validation. See `api/nvidia.com/resource/v1beta1/migconfig.go:44`.\n\n**Kubelet Prepare Path**\n- After the pod lands on the chosen node, kubelet calls the GPU plugin’s prepare path; the driver serializes prepare/unprepare with a node-global lock. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/driver.go:46` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/driver.go:455`.\n- The plugin uses checkpoint state as the source of truth for dynamic MIG recovery and startup reconciliation; on boot it destroys unknown live MIG devices that are not backed by completed checkpoint state. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/driver.go:99` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:405`.\n- Prepare loads the claim allocation, matches each allocated `DeviceName` back to an internal allocatable, applies config, and constructs `PreparedDevices`. For each prepared device it precomputes the CDI qualified name that kubelet will return upward. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:1036`.\n\n**Dynamic MIG Realization**\n- The key transition is from the abstract scheduled partition to a concrete live MIG instance. The docs define this as “create the partition on demand” after scheduler allocation. See `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:87`.\n- The checkpoint stores enough MIG identity to survive restarts, including parent UUID, profile, and placement metadata. The MIG naming model is encoded in canonical names like `gpu-<minor>-mig-<profile>-<profileID>-<placementStart>`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/deviceinfo.go:72` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/mig.go:111`.\n- That means the driver can map the scheduler’s abstract allocation to a specific profile/placement tuple, create it in NVML, then later resolve the live GI/CI minors needed for CDI device-node injection.\n\n**CDI Injection**\n- Once devices are prepared, the plugin writes a **per-claim transient CDI spec** with `CreateClaimSpecFile()`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:181`.\n- The CDI spec class is `claim`, and each device name is qualified as `k8s.gpu.nvidia.com/claim=<claimUID>-<deviceCanonicalName>`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:323`.\n- For a prepared MIG device, CDI generation starts from the parent GPU spec, then appends MIG-specific device nodes for the live GPU Instance and Compute Instance capability files under `/proc/driver/nvidia-caps/...`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:217` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:342`.\n- Prepare returns those CDI device IDs in the prepared result/checkpoint, and the container runtime consumes the transient CDI YAML at start. The docs explicitly call out that CDI injects `nvidia-smi` and host driver libraries into a plain image. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:1052` and `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md:158`.\n\n**Teardown**\n- When the claim is released, kubelet calls unprepare; the driver again takes the global lock and calls `state.Unprepare()`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/driver.go:455`.\n- Unprepare reverses any driver-owned state for the claim, including dynamic MIG cleanup, then deletes the transient CDI file with `DeleteClaimSpecFile()`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:579` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:308`.\n- Finally, it removes the claim entry from the checkpoint so the prepared state no longer exists as recoverable node-local truth. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:589`.\n- There is also periodic cleanup for stale partially prepared claims; if a claim disappeared from the API server, the plugin self-initiates unprepare based on checkpoint evidence. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cleanup.go:117`.\n\n**Evidence to Inspect**\n- **Intent:** `ResourceClaim` / `ResourceClaimTemplate` with `mig.nvidia.com` and optional profile selector, e.g. `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md:170`.\n- **Inventory:** node `ResourceSlice` entries showing MIG `type`, `profile`, `parentUUID`, and dynamic partitionable counters, documented in `site/content/docs/reference/resourceslice-attributes.md:29`.\n- **Allocation:** claim status showing allocated device and node, plus scheduler events.\n- **Prepare:** kubelet-plugin logs around `NodePrepareResources`, checkpoint entries in `checkpoint.json`, and transient CDI YAML creation in the plugin CDI root.\n- **Injection:** returned CDI device IDs from prepare, then container-visible `nvidia-smi -L`.\n- **Teardown:** unprepare logs, CDI file deletion, checkpoint entry removal.\n\nIf you want, I can turn this into a compact “signal-by-signal” troubleshooting checklist for operators following a failing dynamic MIG claim."}}
